Krishna Bhagya Jala Nigam Limited 
6.2 Commencement of works without possession of land 
Commencement of works without possession of required land resulted in 
stoppage of works, rendering the expenditure of ₹ 30.00 crore blocked. 
Paragraph 135(3) of the Karnataka Public Works Departmental Code (KPWD 
Code), stipulates that no work shall be taken up or tenders invited for a work 
without ensuring availability of land. In rare cases, when tenders for works are 
accepted but the lands required for the purpose are still to be acquired, then 
action should be taken on war footing to initiate land acquisition process.  
Paragraph 62 of KPWD Code prescribes that the land was to be acquired under 
the provisions of the relevant Land Acquisition Act 98 or Karnataka Highways 
Act 1964 through Assistant Commissioners of Sub-Divisions or appointment of 
Special Land Acquisition Officers/Highway Authority appointed for the 
process. 
Audit scrutiny of the records of the Office of the Executive Engineer, Krishna 
Bhagya Jala Nigam Limited (KBJNL), Narayanpura Right Bank Canal 
Division-6, Chikkahonnakuni (Division), revealed that in contravention of the 
above codal provisions, the Divisio n had invited tenders (November 2014 to 
December 2017) for four works (one canal extension work and three lateral 
works) costing ₹ 32.91 crore for providing irrigation benefits to the identified 
areas and awarded (April 2015 to March 2018) them without ens uring 
availability of land.  
Audit observed that these four works were partially executed and subsequently 
stopped, since last three to eight years due to non -availability of required land 
as detailed in Appendix - 28. The process of land acquisition for incomplete 
stretches is still under progress (February 2024). The Division had incurred an 
up-to-date expenditure of ₹ 30.00 crore (91 per cent of the tendered cost ) on 
these four works between October 2015 and June 2019. 
The failure on the part of the Division in ensuring availability of land prior to 
invitation of tenders/commencement of works resulted in stoppage of the works 
rendering the expenditure of ₹ 30.00 crore incurred thereon blocked up for 
periods ranging from four to eight years. The non-completion of works not only 
defeated the envisaged objective of pr oviding irrigation benefit to the needy 
areas, but also carries the risk of cost escalation due to time over-run. 
KBJNL in its reply (February 2024) stated that the land acquisition in respect of 
the above four works was pending due to protests from farmers demanding 
change of alignment and enhanced compensation. It was further stated that as 
per the contract conditions the Contractors were also responsible for obtaining 
the possession of land with the consent of farmers and no extra cost was payable 
 
98 From 1 January 2014 under Right to Fair Compensation and Transparency in Land 
Acquisition, Rehabilitation and Resettlement Act 2013.  

Chapter- VI: Compliance Audit Observations on State Public Sector Enterprises 
90 
to them on account of delay in completion of works due to land acquisition 
issues. Government endorsed (April 2024) the reply of KBJNL. 
The reply is not acceptable as the execution of works without taking possession 
of land is in violation of the provisions of KPWD Code. The fact that the 
required land is yet to be acquired even after eight years of commencement of 
works indicated lackadaisical effort by KBJNL. The contention that as per the 
contract conditions, Contractor was also responsible for obtaining possession of 
land is also not acceptable, as the Government/ Department has to acquire the 
required land for public works through Assi stant Commissioners of Sub -
Divisions or appointment of Special Land Acquisition Officers/Highway 
Authorities under the provisions of relevant Land Acquisition Act. Thus, the 
responsibility of making land available for the project lies with the Government 
and not the Contractor. Inaction on the part of KBJNL to acquire required land, 
not only resulted in blocking of funds, but also the envisaged goal of providing 
irrigation facility to the needy beneficiaries remained unachieved. 
 
 
6.3 Undue financial benefit to the Contractor 
Failure to invoke contract condition relating to change in lumpsum price 
of the contract in accordance with change in scope of work resulted in 
undue financial benefit of ₹ 29.77 crore to the Contractor.  
The contract agreement is a legal pact between the Contractor and the Employer 
which defines the scope of work, contract price, terms and conditions including 
those related to quality and cost control during execution of work. The cost 
control condition in the contract defines the changes to be made in contract price 
due to change in scope of work to be carried out. Any deviation from the agreed 
conditions could confer unwarranted financial benefit on the Contractor.  
The Chief Engineer, Krishna Bhagya Jala Nigam Limited (KBJNL), Canal Zone 
No.1, Bheemarayangudi accorded technical sanction (January 2018)  for the 
work of “Survey, Investigation, Design, Supply, Erection, Testing and 
Commissioning of Lift Irrigation System (LIS) for augmentation of filling up 
of 35 Minor Irrigation/Zilla Panchayat tanks in Yadgir District by constructing 
two stage lifts for lifting water fro m Bhima river on Turn -Key basis”. The 
Executive Engineer, KBJNL, Sonthi LIS Division, Khanapur, Yadgi r District 
(Division) invited (December 2017) tenders for the above work at an estimated 
amount of ₹ 433.17 crore . After due tendering process, the work was awarded 
to M/s. Larsen and Toubro Limited, Bengaluru (Contractor) for the contract 
price of ₹ 351.90 crore. KBJNL entered into an agreement with the Contractor 
Recommendation:  
15. Urgent action may be taken to remove the bottlenecks in land 
acquisition and complete the works to provide the envisaged 
irrigation benefit to the needy areas. KBJNL also needs to review 
the contract condition wherein the Contractor is made responsible 
for required land acquisition, as it is contrary to the provisions of 
KPWD Code. 

Audit Report–SPSEs for the year ended 31 March 2023 
91 
on 15 March 2018 with the stipulated date of completion of 24 months i.e., up 
to 14 March 2020. The work was completed and an amount of ₹ 333.50 crore99 
was paid (October 2022) to the Contractor.  
Audit scrutiny (November 2023) of the records of the Division revealed that the 
contract conditions of the work included cost control provision (Clause 32.2) 
which stipulated that “changes in quantity of any item or addition or deletion of 
any item with respect to bill of quantities submitted by the Contractor do not 
affect the total lump sum price, provided there is no change in scope of work 
described in Tender Document or otherwis e provided for in the Contract”. As 
per the agreement, the scope of work, inter alia, included the item “Supplying, 
Laying, Testing and Commissioning of Gravity Distribution Pipeline Network 
of length of 123.59 KM100”. As per the approved price breakup, the Contractor 
was to be paid an amount of ₹ 121.08 crore for the above component of work. 
As per the bid conditions, the Contractor had to get the detailed design and 
drawings approved by the Competent Authority before proceeding with 
execution of the work. Accordingly, the Chief Engineer (CE), KBJNL, Canal 
Zone-1 Bheemarayanagudi approved ( August 2018 ) the final design and 
drawing of the above work, wherein the length of the Gravity Mains was 
reduced from the agreed scope of 123.59 KM to 90.40 KM.  The work was 
executed as per the above approved design and drawing resulting in saving of 
33.19 KM under Gravity Main works. 
As the scope of the work was reduced, the Division was required to exercise the 
contract provision (Clause 32.2) to adjust the contract price proportionate to the 
reduction in scope of work and the resultant savings should have been passed 
on to KBJNL. As per the reduced scope of work, the Contractor was eligible for 
a proportionate payment of ₹ 88.56 crore101 for the component “Gravity Mains”. 
However, the Division failed to invoke the cost control provision of the contract 
and made payment (October 2022) of ₹ 118.33 crore under the component 
“Gravity Mains” as per the original scope of 123.59 KM provided in the 
agreement instead of actual exe cution of 90.40 KM. This resulted in undue 
financial benefit of ₹ 29.77 crore to the Contractor.  
KBJNL replied (April 2024) that the scope of the work was “Filling of 35 tanks” 
which had not been reduced during execution. The design and drawings 
indicated in the tender document were tentative and the working drawings and 
detailed specifications were developed and submitted du ring the execution 
phase by the Contractor in which the length of Gravity Mains was reduced from 
123.59 KM to 90.40 KM. Thus, there was no change in scope and Clause 32.2 
of the tender document could not be operated to reduce the c ontract price. 
Government also endorsed (April 2024) the reply of KBJNL. 
The reply cannot be accepted as the scope of work included in the tender 
document clearly indicated that the length of Gravity Mains to be laid was 
 
99 Excluding price adjustment bills. 
100  96.85 KM under Stage 1 and 26.74 KM under Stage 2. 
101 90.4 km (as per modified scope)/ 123.59 km (as per original scope) × ₹ 121.08 crore (quoted 
price) = ₹ 88.56 crore.

123.59 KM. However, the approval to final design and drawings accorded by 
CE reduced the scope of work by curtailing the length of Gravity Mains to 90.40 
KM. This reduction of more than 26 per cent in scope of works warranted a 
proportionate reduction in payment to the Contractor as per Clause 32.2. 
The failure of KBJNL to invoke the contract condition regarding cost control 
resulted in excess payment of ₹ 29.77 crore and undue financial benefit to the 
Contractor. 
6.4 Excess payment to Contractor 
Adoption of incorrect price indices for regulating price adjustment 
payments resulted in excess payment of ₹ 5.78 crore. 
To address the frequent fluctuations in rates of construction materials and 
labour, Government of Karnataka, issued (November 2004)  order allowing 
price adjustment clause in all works whose estimated cost put to tender was 
₹ 100 lakh or more and the period of completion was 12 months or more. The 
price adjustment was to be computed in accordance with the conditions and 
formulae agreed  in conditions of contract and based on the appropriate 
wholesale price index issued from time to time by the Ministry of Com merce 
and Industry, Government of India.  
Audit scrutiny (June 2023 and August 2023) of the records of two executing 
divisions102 of Krishna Bhagya Jala Nigam Ltd. (KBJNL) revealed excess 
payment to the Contractors due to adoption of inappropriate price indices for 
regulation of price adjustment as detailed below: 
i) Incorrect adoption of price index related to Plant and Machinery 
Spares component. 
Executive Engineer, Narayanpur Right Bank Canal (NRBC) Division No.5,  
KBJNL awarded (July 2021 ) the work of “Extension, Renovation and 
Modernisation of distributaries from 1 to 15 laterals including structures of 
Narayanapura Right Bank Canal System” to a Contractor 103 at the tendered 
cost of ₹ 828.40 crore. The work was under progress and the Contractor had 
achieved financial progress of ₹ 628.40 crore as of March 2023. Additionally, 
the NRBC Division paid (January 2023) ₹ 5.72 crore to the Contractor through 
price adjustment bills including ₹ 1.58 crore on account of “Plant and 
Machinery Spares” component. 
 
102 Narayanpur Right Bank Canal Division No.5 and Indi Branch Canal Operation and 
Maintenance Division, Rampur. 
103 M/s. N. D. Waddar and Company, Lingasugur. 
Recommendation:  
16. KBJNL should take urgent action to recover the excess payment 
duly adhering to the agreed contractual terms and conditions.

As per Clause 44 of the General Conditions of Contract, the price adjustment 
for “Plants and Machinery Spares ” component was to be regulated based on 
the all-India average wholesale price index for “Material Handling, Lifting and 
Hoisting Equipment ”. However, the NRBC Division regulated the price 
adjustment claim for the component “Plants and Machinery Spares ” 
considering the average wholesale price index of “Manufacture of Machinery 
and Equipment” instead of “Material Handling, Lifting and Hoisting 
Equipment” specified in the contract agreement resulting in excess payment of 
₹ 1.15 crore. (Appendix - 29). 
ii) Incorrect adoption of price index related to steel component 
Executive Engineer, Indi Branch Canal (IBC) Operation and Maintenance 
Division, Rampur entrusted (January 2021)  the work of “Restoration and 
providing mechanical gates to the Umarani Barrage constructed across Bhima 
River for augmentation of water to Chadchan Lift irrigation scheme’’ to a 
Contractor104 at the contract price of ₹ 133.33 crore after due tender process.  
The work was under progress and the Contractor was paid ₹ 133.46 crore apart 
from ₹ 24.91 crore towards price adjustment of March 2023.  
Clause 44 of the General conditions of Contract stipulated that in respect of 
Steel component, the All -India average wholesale price index for 
reinforcement and structural steel  was to be adopted for regulating price 
adjustment. However, the IBC Division, Rampur adopted the sub-group “Mild 
Steel-Flat Products” under “Manufacture of Basic Metals” instead of the sub-
group “Mild Steel Flats & Sheets” under “Structural Steel Component”  for 
price adjustment of steel flat products. This resulted in excess payment to the 
tune of ₹ 5.87 crore as detailed in Appendix - 30. 
KBJNL in its reply (January 2024) stated that an amount of ₹ 1.25 crore was 
recovered (November 2023) towards incorrect adoption of price index related 
to “Plant and Machinery Spares” component from the Contractor and further 
submitted that action for recovery of ₹ 5.87 crore towards price adjustment of 
steel component was initiated. Government endorsed (January 2024) the reply 
of KBJNL. 
Thus, the adoption of inappropriate indices for price adjustment in respect of 
“Plant and Machinery” and “Steel Flats” components in two works resulted in 
excess payment to the Contractors to the tune of ₹ 7.02 crore out of which 
recovery of an amount of ₹ 5.78 crore was still pending (May 2024).

Recommendation:  
17. KBJNL should take action to recover the excess amount paid to the 
Contractors and give direction to all Divisions to scrupulously 
adhere to the contract agreement conditions while regulating price 
adjustment payments.

6.5 Fraudulent payment for works not executed 
False certification and fraudulent payment of ₹ 33.95 lakh was made for 
drain work which was not executed. 
Paragraph 110.1(a) of the Karnataka Public Works Departmental Code 
(KPWD Code) stipulates that the field engineer in charge of a work should 
conduct measurements for all works executed in accordance with 
sanctioned plans, estimates and prescribed specifications. Furthe r, 
Paragraph 110.8 of the KPWD Code mandates that measurements initially 
recorded by the field engineer shall be check measured by the Assistant 
Executive Engineer (AEE). One of the objectives of check measurement is 
to detect errors in measurement and to prevent fraudulent entries. After 
check measurement, the AEE shall record in his handwriting under his 
signature with date that the recorded measurements are correct and that 
the works are carried out as per sanctioned plans and approved 
specifications. A false certification, either by the field engineer or the AEE, 
can be construed as a fraudulent attempt to facilitate payment from the 
Government by unfair means and invites penal action. 
The work of road formation from Jogundabhavi to Kamalapur in 
Shorapur taluk, Yadgir District was taken up by Executive Engineer (EE), 
Krishna Bhagya Jala Nigama Ltd (KBJNL) Dam Division, Narayanapura 
(Division) at the estimated cost of ₹ 1.60 crore. The work was awarded 
(March 2019) to a Contractor 105 after due tender process for the contract 
amount of ₹ 1.48 crore  with the stipulated completion period of four 
months. The work was completed on 04 July 2019 at the cost of ₹ 1.63 crore. 
Audit scrutiny of records of the Division (July 2023) revealed that the work 
included inter alia the following items as per the approved Bill of Quantities 
(BoQ) in the Table below:

Thus, even though the item of work “Excavation for drains” was not 
executed, it was recorded in the measurement book that 22,000 cum of 
earth work excavation  had been carried out. AEE also certified that 100 
per cent check measurements  were carried out and accordingly payment 


Chapter- VI: Compliance Audit Observations on State Public Sector Enterprises 
96 
was made. This resulted in payment of ₹ 33.95 106 lakh for an item of work 
which had not been executed. The manipulation of the r ecordings in the 
measurement book and false certification by the officials resulted in 
fraudulent payment of ₹ 33.95 lakh to the Contractor. 
KBJNL in its reply (April 2024) accepted that the item of work “Excavation 
for drains” was not executed and that the provision in the estimates for the 
above item was utilised for removing the surface boulders and hard rock 
to make the road surface even and uniform. It was also stated that no 
fictious measurements have been recorded and it was only a procedural 
irregularity in operating the provision available for “Excavation for 
drains” for another essential item of work. It was also assured that a 
detailed enquiry would be conducted to ascertain and recover the excess 
payment, if any, and to fix responsibility on the Officers concerned. 
Government endorsed (April 2024) the reply of KBJNL. 
The reply cannot be accepted as 22,000 cum of earthwork in respect of the 
item “Excavation for drains” had been falsely certified by departmental 
officer as executed in the measurement book and payment of ₹ 33.95 lakh 
was made on the above basis. The Divis ion had neither proposed nor 
received approval from the competent authority for executing the extra 
item “Removing surface boulders and hard rock” and hence payment 
made on this account was fraudulent.

failed to take note of the proposed substation and raise objections as was 
appropriate.

of the allotted land before acquiring it and starting construction of the substation 
considering the facts available on hand, led to an additional expenditure of 
₹ 5.35 crore towards protection works to prevent the site from submergence. As 
the site is low lying and walled up on three sides, there may be chances of 
flooding in the sub -station during the rainy season.  Further, the Company has 
to, time and again, pump out seepage water and rain water from the sub-station 
to keep it safe.  
6.7 Avoidable payment of interest  
Delay in decision making at various stages resulted in avoidable payment 
of interest of ₹ 3.97 crore 
Land measuring 12 acres 37 guntas at Bidadi Industrial Area was handed over 
during March 1998 by Karnataka Industrial Area Development Board (KIADB) 
to Karnataka Power Transmission Corporation Limited (the Company) . The 
total cost of the land was fixed at ₹  1.93 crore at the rate of ₹  15 lakh per acre 
as decided (December 1997) in the meeting held under the chairmanship of the 
Principal Secretary to the Government of Karnataka, Commerce and Industries 
Department. The Company established the 220kV sub-station in October 1998 
and paid the total cost of the land to the Board in October 2004.  
As the Company delayed making payment towards the cost of the land, KIADB 
demanded (January 2005) interest of ₹ 2.09 crore for the period from 1 March 
1998 to 30 November 2004. The Company’s request (October 2005) for waiver 
of interest was rejected (April 2008) by the KIADB. The entire interest amount 
of ₹ 2.09 crore was paid by the Company in February 2019. Meanwhile, KIADB 
raised (January 2019) a revised demand of ₹ 5.67 crore towards interest for the 
period from 1 March 1998 to 31 January 2019, as the Company delayed the 
payment of interest. However, considering the request of the Company, KIADB 
Recommendation:  
19. Audit recommends the management to fix responsibility on the 
delinquent officials for their failure to adhere to the CEA 
regulations while selecting site for substation. 

Chapter- VI: Compliance Audit Observations on State Public Sector Enterprises 
100 
waived off 50 per cent of the further interest component for the period from 1 
December 2004 to 31 January 2019.  Accordingly, the remaining interest of 
₹ 1.88 crore 109 was paid by the Company  in February 2020.  As a result, the 
Company had ended up paying interest of ₹ 3.97 crore, which was more than 
double the cost of the land (₹ 1.93 crore). 
In the light of above facts, the Audit observed the following: 
• The Company requested KIADB in March 2001  for exemption from 
payment of land cost citing higher interest on loan obtained for 
establishment of substation and low utilization of the substation 
capacity, which was rejected by KIADB in June 2001. The Company’s 
requests to negotiate with KIADB for reduction of price by 50 per cent, 
in March 2002 and subsequently in June 2004 were also rejected. In the 
process, more than six years had elapsed by the time the Company made 
the payment of land cost to KIADB (March 1998 to October 2004).  In 
view of approval of 50 per cent payment by the corporate office of the 
company in March 2002 and constant rejection by KIADB to reduce the 
price of the land, the delay in payment of the cost of land to KIADB was 
unwarranted. 
• The approval for payment of interest amount  was accorded by the 
Director (Administration & Human Resource) in May 2008 and the 
budget was allocated in October 2008. However, the payment was made 
only in February 2019 , i.e. more than ten years after the approval for 
payment. Audit observed that the Chief Engineer, Electricity, 
Transmission Zone, Bangalore had urged ( September 2009 and April 
2010) the Financial Advisor (Accounts & Resources) for arranging 
payment of interest to KIADB. Despite these requests no action was 
taken to arrange the payment.  This warrants fixing of responsibility on 
the concerned for the lapses, as this inaction led to payment of further 
interest of ₹  1.88 crore for the period from 1 December 2004 to 31 
January 2019. While the financial implication of interest payment was 
deliberated (March 2020) by the Company’s Board of Directors, the 
issue of delays in releasing the payment leading to levy of additional 
interest was not included in the Agenda for the Board meeting and was, 
therefore, not discussed. The additional interest for the period from 1 
December 2004 to 31 January 2019 could have been avoided by the 
Company, had it acted promptly in payment of dues after the rejection 
of interest waiver request by KIADB in April 2008. 
Thus, due to delay in decision -making and payment settlement, the Company 
took an unjustifiably long period of 22 years for settling (2019-20) a transaction 
that was initiated during 1997 -98. This resulted in additional outgo of ₹ 3.97 
crore for the Compan y, which was avoidable.  Further, these inefficiencies 
would ultimately result in burdening the consumers, as such costs are factored 
into the tariff.  
 
109 ₹ 185.80 lakh (50 per cent of balance interest of ₹ 371.60 lakh) plus EMD - ₹ 0.65 lakh plus 
Slum Clearance charges - ₹ 1.32 lakh. 

Audit Report–SPSEs for the year ended 31 March 2023 
101 
The Company stated (July 2023)  that the delay in submission of proposal for 
payment was attributable to the earnest efforts made by the Company for getting 
exemption from payment of land cost . The reply is not acceptable as after the 
initial rejection by KIADB for waiver of payment, the Company could have 
paid 50 per cent of the land cost to avoid accumulation of interest. Further, the 
Company in its reply has claimed that even though the budget was released for 
payment of interest of ₹ 2.09 crore during October 2008, the payment was made 
during February 2019 as the Company was constantly pursuing with KIADB to 
reconsider the rejection of the proposal for waiver of interest amount. The claim 
of the Company is not acceptable as the claim is not substantiated by any 
documentary proof. Also, the approval for payment of interest of ₹ 2.09 crore 
(May 2008) was given only after rejection by KIADB for waiver during April 
2008.  
Government stated (January 2024) that the Company had installed E -office 
software wherein every file movement could be tracked and delays in movement 
are monitored. The Government has also stated that as the subject matter is more 
than 15 years old and the incumbent officers have already retired from 
service/died, no action can be initiated for the lapses, if any,  at this length of 
time. 
The reply is not acceptable as the persons responsible for the lapses in making 
the payment in time were not identified and presented to the board while 
presenting the financial implication in March 2020 or at a later stage.  
6.8 Idle expenditure and loss 
The lapses at various stages resulted in a 15 -year delay in project 
completion, a decade of idling costly equipment and loss of ₹ 3.16 crore. 
In December 2006, the Karnataka Power Transmission Corporation Limited 
(the Company) sanctioned the Detailed Project Report (DPR)  for the 
establishment of a 110/33/11kV substation at Jeppu in Mangalore Taluk. 
Additionally, it involved constructing a 110kV LILO 110 line spanning 10 
kilometers from the existing Kulashekara -Konaje Single Circuit line to the 
proposed substation at Jeppu. The project aimed to alleviate loads in existing 
33kV substations, minimize energy losses on 11kV feeders and reduce power 
interruptions while regulating 11kV line tail-end voltage in the substation area. 
Following a tender process, the Company awarded the contract (January 2008) 
to M/s. Deepak Cables (India) Limited (contractor) at a total cost of ₹ 9.01 
crore111. The stipulated completion time for substation and line works was seven 
and six months, respectively, from the date of the Letter of Intent ( 29 January 
 
110 LILO- Line in line out- an arrangement for tapping an existing transmission line to serve a 
new sub-station. 
111 Supply of material (₹ 663.46 lakh); Erection (₹ 20.06 lakh); Civil works (₹ 217.63 lakh). 
Recommendation:  
20. Audit recommends that role-based timelines may be fixed for such 
transactions to avoid similar lapses in the future.

102 
2008). Non -compliance with the contract terms could result in performance 
guarantee forfeiture, penalty imposition, and contract termination, as per the 
General Conditions of Contract (Clause 45.3). 
The contractor failed to meet the specified deadlines, leading to the termination 
of the contract in May 2016, following a final notice issued in March 2016. At 
the time of termination, the contractor had partially completed the work 112 for 
which the Company paid ₹  5.97 crore to the contractor . The Company held 
₹ 3.29 crore from the contractor’s bill , comprising Retention Money (₹  1.31 
crore), Liquidated Damages (₹ 94.79 lakh), and encashment of Bank Guarantee 
(₹ 1.03 crore). Subsequently, the remaining work was re-tendered and awarded 
to another firm113 in September 2019, which was completed (January 2023) at 
a cost of ₹ 4.18 crore114. 
In this regard, Audit observed the following: 
i) Despite the contractor's lack of progress since July 2013 and a complete 
halt from May 2015, the decision to terminate the contract was delayed 
until May 2016. The Company, during the intervening period, engaged 
with the contractor but failed to terminate the contract promptly as per 
terms of the contract.  
ii) The Company took over three years to re-award the contract (September 
2019) after termination.  Delay in estimate preparation  and multiple 
tender invitations contributed to the prolonged timeline , rendering a 
significant expenditure of ₹ 7.02 crore115 unproductive.  
iii) Based on the request (May 2016) of the Company , the contractor 
appointed (July 2016) its representative  for taking joint inventory and 
also returned the unutilized material relating to the line works. As per 
the terms of contract, the contractor was responsible for the safety of all 
the equipments/materials under custody.  The contractor requested 
(February 2017) the Company to make necessary arrangements to 
safeguard the assets at the substation. However, the Company appointed 
the security agency only in November 2017, 18 months after termination 
and found that the materials116 worth ₹  3.16 crore  were 
missing/damaged. Further, as per the termination of contract notice 
dated May 2016, the work was deemed to have been taken  over by the 
Company. The Company neglected its obligation to take over the assets 
immediately after contract termination, leading to a loss of ₹ 3.16 crore. 
The delay in appointing a security agency allowed for the 
disappearance/damage of materials including two transformers costing 
₹ 2 crore, which were found unsuitable for repair. Though the Company

Government replied (March 2024) that delay in works was due to many reasons 
such as objections from land owners in line corridors, court cases and also, delay 
by the agency . Further, as of November 2015, 70 per cent  of the work was 
completed by the contractor and based on the request of the contractor to not 
short close the contract and long business association that the contractor 
maintained with the Company, termination of contract was delayed. The process 
of termination of contract was also delayed to avoid time and cost involved in 
re-tendering process. The reply is not acceptable as work was awarded during 
2008, and the stipulated ti me period was six and seven months respectively.  
However, the Company allowed th e contractor to continue much after the 
stipulated time period.  
Further, with respect to the delay in re -award of the contract for the balance 
works, the Government replied that the delay was due to multiple revisions in 
the estimates prepared due to introduction of Goods and Service Tax  and 
damage/loss of materials . The delay could have been avoided as the action 
involved was entirely within the purview of the Company. The process of 
preparation of estimates and its technical sanction consumed unreasonable time 
of more than two years from the date of termination (May 2 016 to July 2018) 
which is not justified. Further, the reply is silent on exactly how the introduction 
of GST caused multiple revisions in the estimate. 
Regarding the loss of materials worth ₹ 3.16 crore, the Government replied that 
the contractor was responsible for the safety of the materials until they are 
returned to store authorities as per the Joint Inventory statement . Also, a 
complaint had been lodged against the contractor under relevant sections of IPC 
and the investigation is under progress . The reply cannot be accepted as when 
the contract was terminated, the works were deemed to be taken over by the 
authorized representative of KPTCL as stated in the termination order.  In 
addition, the Company should have been prompt in appointing a security agency 
to safeguard the assets when the contractor had communicated its inability to 
provide security at the premises.  
The lapses at various stages resulted in a 15-year delay in project completion, a 
decade of idling costly equipment and a loss of ₹ 3.16 crore. The Company has 

Chapter- VI: Compliance Audit Observations on State Public Sector Enterprises 
104 
to take necessary action to address these issues and fix responsibility for such 
negligence. 
Visvesvaraya Jala Nigam Limited  
6.9 Excess payment of electricity charges 
Failure of VJNL to verify the correctness of the electricity bills resulted in 
excess payment of electricity charges amounting to ₹ 16.89 crore. 
The Karnataka Electricity Regulatory Commission (KERC) issues tariff orders 
time to time that provide guidelines for the calculation of energy charges. As 
per the tariff orders, the Government owned Corporations operating Lift 
Irrigation Schemes (LIS) were obligated to pay the prescribed annual minimum 
charges, if the annual energy charges fell below the minimum prescribed 
threshold. The minimum electricity charges for LIS owned by Government -
owned Corporations were to be determined on an annual basis, considering the 
installed capacity of the pump house.  
The prescribed energy charges and minimum tariff charges for LIS implemented 
by Government owned corporations is as listed below: 
Table No. 6.9.1: Electricity tariff for LIS schemes

tariff order

Per HP/ annum 
3 2021-22 310 Paise per unit subject to annual minimum of ₹ 1720 
Per HP/ annum 
4 2022-23 315 Paise per unit subject to annual minimum of ₹ 1900 
Per HP/ annum 
Source: KERC Orders 
Executive Engineer (EE), Upper Bhadra Project Division No 2, Visvesvaraya 
Jala Nigam Limited (VJNL) was operating two pump houses at Shanthipura and 
Bettatavarekere. These pump houses were utilised for lifting water from the 
Bhadra reservoir to the deliver y chamber located near the proposed tunnel at 
Ajjampura as part of the Upper Bhadra Project. The pump houses at Shanthipura 
and Bettatavarekere were commissioned in February 2019 and August 2019  
respectively.  
Audit scrutiny (September 2021) of the electricity bills of the office of the Chief 
Engineer, Upper Bhadra Project Zone, VJNL revealed that the Mangalore 
Recommendation:  
21. Audit recommends the management to fix responsibility on the 
officials for not taking over the assets on termination of the contract 
leading to loss of assets.

Electricity Supply Company (MESCOM) calculated the energy bills of the 
pump houses on monthly basis by dividing KERC prescribed annual minimum 
charges by twelve against which the actual energy charges for the month was 
compared with. The higher among the two charges was considered in the 
monthly bills of the pump houses. Audit compared the annual energy charges 
based on actual consumption, KERC prescribed minimum annual charges and

As observed above, for both the pump houses, the annual energy charges based 
on actual consumption was less than the annual minimum prescribed charges 
for all the financial years during the period 2019-23. During 2022-23, the actual 
energy charges levied by MESCOM was less than the annual minimum charges. 
As per the KERC tariff order, the annual minimum charges based on the 
installed capacity was to be levied as the electricity charges for the respective 
years. Since the electricity charges were calculated monthly without complying 
to the KERC order prescribing adoption of annual minimum charges, VJNL 
made excess payment of ₹ 16.89 crore on account of electricity charges during 
2019-20 to 2022-23 (Details vide Appendix - 31 and Appendix - 32). 
Thus, the incorrect compliance by MESCOM to the KERC tariff orders 
regarding LIS schemes and the failure of VJNL to verify the correctness of the 
MESCOM bills resulted in excess payment of electricity charges to the tune of 
₹ 16.89 crore. 
VJNL in its reply ( November 2023) accepted the audit observation and stated 
that MESCOM has agreed (November 2023) to withdraw the excess billing and 
adjust the excess amount of ₹ 24.22 crore117 (along with interest of ₹ 2.57 crore) 
from the future bills of the pump houses at Shantipura a nd Bettavarekere. 
Government endorsed (December 2023) the reply of VJNL. 
 
117 As calculated by MESCOM for the period up to October 2023.

Gulbarga Electricity Supply Company Limited 
6.10 Undue favour to Contractors  
The Central Purchase Committee of Gulbarga Electricity Supply Company 
Limited had approved refund of Liquidated Damages of ` 10.26 crore without 
any valid grounds and ignoring the delays attributable to the contractors, 
resulting in extension of undue benefit.   
Gulbarga Electricity Supply Company Limited (the Company) awarded various 
system improvement works during 2011-12 and 2012 -13 under the 
Central/State sponsored schemes 118, and also through its Capex Budget. The 
scope of works mainly comprised Replacement of Overhead Lines by LT AB 
Cables, Reconductoring of 11kV lines, Installation of additional Distribution 
Transformer Centers, Replacement of existing meters with Electrostatic Meters, 
Construction of substations and lines, and Construction of agriculture feeders. 
The details of works are given in Appendix - 33.   
The contracts for execution of the above works under the jurisdiction of three 
divisions of the Company (Gulbarga City, Gulbarga and Bidar) were awarded 
to M/s Larsen & Toubro (RAPDRP - March 2012), M/s Ramlingam 
Construction Company (NJY - July 2011/March  2012) and M/s Sharavathy 
Conductors Pvt Ltd, Bangalore (Capex - March 2016) at a total cost of ₹ 172.38 
crore, with a stipulation to complete within 11 months, 6 months and 9 months 
respectively from the date of issue of Letters of Intent. The works were,  
however, completed during 2015 -16 and 2018-19 with delay ranging from 17 
months to 38 months beyond the stipulated dates in the contracts.   
The Company deducted the Liquidated Damages (LD) of ₹ 10.26 crore from 
the bills of the agencies for the delay in completion of works as per the terms of 
contract. Subsequently, based on the request of the agencies, the entire amount 
of LD was refunded by condoning the delay with the approval (2020-21) of the 
Central Purchase Committee (CPC) of the Company.   
The delay in completion of works under NJY and R -APDRP were commented 
in the Audit Report of CAG of India on PSUs, Government of Karnataka for 
the year ended 31 March 2015 and 31 March 2016 respectively.  Audit had 
further scrutinized (2022 -23) the proceed ings of the CPC and the grounds on 
which the LD was refunded to the agencies.   
 
118 Restructured Accelerated Power Development Reforms Programme (R -APDRP) and

Recommendation:  
22. VJNL should review the correctness of electricity bills in respect of

Audit Report–SPSEs for the year ended 31 March 2023 
107 
Audit noted that the grounds on which the decision was taken to condone the 
delay and refund the LD were not justified and contrary to the facts available on 
record.  The detailed audit remarks against each of the reasons given by the CPC 
for condonation of delay are given in the Appendix - 34.  The delays attributed 
to the agencies, though noted by the CPC, were ignored while approving the 
refund of LD, despite having clear evidence of default by the agencies.  Further, 
the CPC while condoning the delays, relied upon factors that did not contribute 
to delay in completion of works.  
• For instance, in respect of e xecution of RAPDRP Part B works 119, 
‘entrustment of additional works’ and ‘reduction in scope’ were 
considered to be reasons for delays, though in reality, they did not 
actually affect the original scope of work. Further, the delay in supply 
of meters, which was considered as prime factor for condonation had 
affected only one of the four components of the said contract, while the 
delay in completion of the remaining was not discussed by the CPC.    
• Similarly in respect of NJY works120, the CPC noted that completion of 
16 out of 24 feeders in Bidar, Division-I and II divisions, which had no 
issues of railway crossings, had been delayed ranging from two months 
to three years from the target date.  LD levied was refunded without any 
valid recorded reasons, even though delays attributable to the agenc ies 
were identified. 
• In case of establishing GIS substations 121, the CPC approved refund of 
LD by overlooking the facts available on record. Several lapses on the 
part of the agency reported by the Executive Engineer (Electrical) of 
C&M Division, Gulbarga, were ignored while condoning the delay. The 
lapses included insufficient manpower deployment, delayed submission 
of drawings, delay in joint inspection, non-payment of statutory fee, lack 
of work progress even in places having no right of way issues, etc.  It is 
evident that the CPC did not take cognizance of these lapses. 
• The LD included both on account of delay in supply of materials and 
erection. An amount of ₹ 2.90 crore122, which was deducted on account 
of delay in supply, was refunded to the agencies. There was neither any 
evidence on record, nor were the reasons assessed by the CPC for the 
delay in supply of materials.

• The delay condonation and LD refund was decided (August 
2020/November 2020) after lapse of more than four to five years of 
completion (February 2015/December 2015/March 2016) of works 
executed by M/s Larsen & Toubro Limited and M/s Ramlingam 
Construction Company Pvt Ltd.  Audit observed that the rationale 
behind the CPC’s decision to take up the subject after such time lapse 
lacked justification.  
The Board of Directors of the Company, while noting the decisions of CPC, 
observed (November 2020) that there was inordinate delay in providing 
materials by the Company causing delay in execution of works.  Directions were 
issued to the Managing Director to apply prudent checks before approving any 
condonation of delay and to issue appropriate Rules 123 and procedures to deal 
with the cases of extension of time and refund of LD.  Audit observed that the 
Board of Directors noted primarily the delay in supply of materials by the 
Company, without going into the veracity of the decision of CPC.  Despite 
emphasizing the need for prudent checks to condone the delay, the Board did 
not deliberate the delays attributed to agencies and whether such prudent checks 
were carried out while approving for refund of LD by the CPC.   
Thus, refund of the entire amount initially recovered from the bills of the  
contractors towards liquidated damages, ignoring the delay attributable to the 
contractors was not prudent and was in violation of conditions of contract. The 
Company should have assessed the liquidated damages recoverable as per terms 
of the contract, in respect of t he original work awarded, duly considering the 
delays attributable to the contractor.  
Government’s reply (March 2024) reiterated the issues deliberated in CPC 
proceedings, which were already considered by Audit. The reply did not address 
the specific issues pointed out by Audit.  
 
 
KPC Gas Power Corporation Limited  
6.11 Avoidable extra expenditure 
Failure to re-assess the requirement of start-up power resulted in avoidable 
expenditure of ₹ 10.17 crore. 
KPC Gas Power Corporation Limited (Company), a fully owned subsidiary of 
Karnataka Power Corporation Limited (KPCL), entrusted (November 2015) the 
work of establishing 1×370 MW Yelahanka Combined Cycle Power Plant 
(YCCPP) at Yelahanka to M/s. Bharat Heavy Electricals Limited (BHEL) at an 
estimated project cost of ₹ 1,571.18 crore.  The contract involved Engineering, 
 
123 The circular to that effect was issued during April 2021. 
Recommendation:  
23. Audit recommends that management should consider conditions of 
contract while refunding Liquidated Damages. 

Audit Report–SPSEs for the year ended 31 March 2023 
109 
Procurement and Construction (EPC) with a scheduled completion period of 30 
months, i.e., by May 2018. 
The Company requested (March 2018)  the Bengaluru Electricity Supply 
Company Limited (BESCOM), for start-up Power124 of 20,000 KV A at voltage 
of 220 KV required for initial trial operation before synchronisation of YCCPP. 
The unit was scheduled to be commissioned during March/April 2018.  
BESCOM gave approval  (August 2018) for start -up power supply on Extra 
High Tension (EHT) basis to be evacuated through 220 KV line from the 
adjacent Puttenahalli Sub -station to the plant premises. The Company paid 
(October 2018) initial security deposit  amounting to ₹ 4.42 crore and entered 
(December 2018) into an agreement with BESCOM  for a contract demand of 
20,000 KV A. The Agreement was valid initially for a period of two years from 
the date of commencement of supply and would stand automatically renewed 
from year to year thereafter, until expressly terminated in writing by either of 
the parties. As per the prevailing tariff, demand charges are levied on actual 
maximum demand recorded in a month or 85 per cent  of contract demand, 
whichever is higher along with the charges for actual consumption of energy at 
rates applicable (HT 2 (b) tariff) from time to time.  
The project which was scheduled for completion by May 2018, was not put into 
operation even as of September 2023 due to various reasons like gas availability 
etc.. The undue delay in project completion was reported vide Paragraph 
2.2.7.1 of Compliance Audit Report for the year 2021. 
During the period from December 2018 to March 2023, the YCCPP plant 
incurred an expenditure of ₹ 36.84 crore towards electricity charges under the 
start-up power supply to run the auxiliaries, without actually starting generation 
of electricity. The maximum demand recorded during the above period ranged 
between 720 to 8,880 KV A (3.6 per cent to 44.4 per cent of Contract Demand). 
However, as per the prevailing tariff , demand charges were payable at actual 
recorded demand or 85 per cent of contract demand, whichever is higher, at the 
rates specified in tariff and the billing was done at 85 per cent of contract 
demand i.e. 17,000 KV A.  
Conditions of supply of Electricity of distribution licences (Sub clause 2 of Rule 
34) provides for reduction/variation in Contract Demand during agreement 
period by executing a fresh agreement.  The reduction would be effective from 
the meter reading date following the expiry of two months period from date of 
registration of application for reduction of contract demand/sanctioned load 
along with fresh agreement. Despite low utilisation of power, the Company did 
not use this provision to reduce its contract demand. Due to non -reduction of 
contract demand, Company incurred an avoidable expenditure of ₹ 10.17 
crore125 as detailed in Appendix - 35.  
 
124 Power required for running the Auxiliary equipment for commissioning activities of a new 
Generating Station.  
125 Calculated by taking 11,000 KVA (maximum consumption plus 20 per cent cushion, 
rounded off to 11,000 KVA) as required contract demand based on the highest demand 
recorded during startup operation in October 2020. 

Chapter- VI: Compliance Audit Observations on State Public Sector Enterprises 
110 
Government forwarded (January 2024 ) the reply of the Company  (January 
2024) which stated that due to various reasons attributable to M/s. BHEL, the 
commissioning activities in full scale had not been taken up. Due to this, the 
startup power could not be utilised in full capacity.  Further, due to uncertainty 
about date of synchronisation of the plant to the State grid, the contract demand 
varied implying unpredictable startup activities. The reply is not acceptable, as 
the Company did not re -assess the requirement of startup power even though 
the date of synchronisation was uncertain due to non-availability of gas. In this 
scenario, Company could have opted for temporary reduction of contract 
demand, which could have avoided expenditure on demand charges.  
Thus, non-reduction of contract demand resulted in avoidable expenditure of 
₹ 10.17 crore, besides burdening the consumers, as the plant is yet to be 
capitalised and additional cost incurred during construction may be factored into 
tariff. 
Karnataka Neeravari Nigam Limited 
6.12 Irregular advance payment on the basis of fake invoices 
The invoices submitted by the Contractor in support of supply of pipes at 
work site did not match with the GST records of the supplier and 
purchaser. The vehicles claimed to have been used for transportation of 
pipes were found to be two wheelers/three wheelers and light motor 
vehicles. The above evidence indicated that the advance payment of ₹ 40.16 
crore was made without verifying supply of materials by the officials of 
VJNL. The irregular advance payment resulted in loss of interest to the 
tune of ₹ 4.53 crore to KNNL. 
The work of “Design, Supply, Installation, Testing and Commissioning of 
filling up of 48 MI/ZP tanks on Turn -Key basis” at Shiggaon in Haveri 
District and Kundagol in Dharwad District under Savanur Lift Irrigation 
Scheme (LIS) was administratively approved  (February 2017)  by 
Karnataka Neeravari Nigam Limited (KNNL). The technical sanction for 
the work was accorded (April 2017) by Chief Engineer, Malprabha Project 
Zone, KNNL, Dharwad for ₹ 97 crore. After due tendering process, the 
Executive Engineer, KNNL S higgaon Lift Irrigation Division, Dharwad 
(Division) awarded (January 2018) the work to M/s. Ocean Constructions 
(India) Private Limited, Mangaluru (Contractor) for a contract price of ₹ 
90.57 crore. An agreement was entered into with the Contractor on 11 
January 2018 with the stipulated period of completion of 18 months i.e. up 
to 10 July 2019. The Managing Director, KNNL granted (January 2024) 
extension of time for completion of the project up to 31 March 2022. 
However, the work was under progress (Septem ber 2023) and no further 
extension was granted thereafter. An up-to-date payment of ₹ 83.20 crore 
was made to the Contractor as of March 2022. 
Recommendation:  
24. Audit recommends the management to explore all avenues towards 
cost reduction while implementing projects.

The Contractor purchased the HDPE pipes from the Manufacturer127 and 
pre-dispatch joint inspection 128 was carried out by the Division at the 
Manufacturer’s site (Rajkot) on 12 July 2018. Thereafter, on 16 July 2018, 
the contractor claimed that all the HDPE pipes have been supplied and 
stored in his stock yard at worksite. Accordingly, the Division made the 
payment of ₹ 40.16 crore for supply of pipes ( i.e. 70 per cent  of the 
completed item of work) vide First Running Account (RA) bill on 07 August 
2018. The remaining items of work such as (i) laying, jointing, testing of 
pipes and (ii) Commissioning were done subsequently between September 
2018 and August 2021. 
The detailed scrutiny of the Measurement Book/First RA bill revealed that 
the payment made to the Contractor by the Division on account of supply 
of pipes at work site was irregular on the following grounds: 
(1) The Contractor submitted 69 tax invoices  in support of pipe 
purchase issued by the Distributor/Supplier 129 giving the details

(such as seller’s/buyer’s details, date/value of transaction,

the tax invoices did not match either with the details of GSTR-I130 
of the supplier or GSTR-2A131 of purchaser. Out of 69 invoices, 52

revealed that out of 69 invoices, 32 vehicle numbers were pertaining

not genuine.

114.03 km length in the measurement book on 16 July 2018  itself which 
formed the basis for payment to the Contractor. Audit observed that the 
recordings made in the measurement book on 16 July 2018 (details such as 
diameter, length and quantity of pipes) did not match with the recordings 
carried out in the measurement book at the time of laying (September 2018 
to August 2021) of those pipes during execution. (Appendix - 37) 
The above evidence clearly indicated that the Contractor submitted fake 
invoices and the departmental officers extended help to the Contractor to 
get undue financial benefit of one -time advance payment of ₹ 40.16 crore

Karnataka State Handicrafts Development Corporation Limited 
6.13 Non-achievement of objectives  
Inordinate delay in completion of construction of a multi -utility complex 
in Peenya Industrial Area and construction of a Community Centre and 
renovation of a temple in Shivarapatna, Kolar District in spite of incurring 
an expenditure of ₹ 1.28 crore resulted in non -achievement of the 
objectives.  
I. Karnataka State Handicrafts Development Corporation Limited (Company) 
owned a building constructed (1990)  in Peenya Industrial Area to house a 
Bronze Craft Complex for the benefit of Artisans. The building has not been 
utilized since 1995 as the production of Bronze Articles was stopped on account 
of the distance factor as the craft persons were scattered at various locations in 
Bangalore.  
The Board of Directors (BoD) of the Company, in its 286th meeting, approved 
(July 2017 ) the demolition of existing structure and decided to construct a 
multi-utility complex including warehouse, exhibition centre, office space, etc. 
The project was to be funded by the Company’s own resources . It was 
envisaged that ₹ 50.68 lakh, being the annual rent for the warehouse, could be 
saved annually. BoD authorised  the Managing Director to entrust the 
construction work either through tender process or through Karnataka Rural 
Infrastructure Development Limited (KRIDL). KRIDL submitted (October 
2017) seven estimates at a total cost of ₹ 8.84 crore splitting the construction of 
complex into seven works137.   
Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) was signed (March 2018) with KRIDL 
for the basement floor work (₹ 1.78 crore). As per the MoU, 50 per cent of the 
estimate was to be paid on entering the MoU, 25 per cent was to be paid after 
completion of 40 per cent  of the work, 20 per cent  was to be paid after 
completion of 60 per cent of the work and the balance 5 per cent was to be paid 
after completion of the work. The work was to be completed within six months 
from the date of handing over the land. As per the MoU, ₹  88.88 lakh (50 per 
cent of the cost) was released in March 2018. Approval for the plan was 
obtained from Bruhat Benagaluru Mahanagara Palike in September 2018  and 
the site was handed over in September 2018 to KRIDL . The work was to be 
completed by March 2019. However, the work is yet to be completed (February 
2024).  
In this connection, Audit observed that: 
• GoK had granted exemption under 4(g)  of Karnataka Transparency in 
Public Procurement (KTPP) Act (July 2016 and April 2017) to KRIDL. 
According to this, Public Sector Undertakings, Companies, 
Departments etc. could directly entrust works up to ₹ 2 crore to KRIDL 
 
137 Basement Floor: ₹ 177.75 lakh; Ground Floor: ₹ 178.10 lakh; First Floor: ₹ 103.40 lakh; 
Second Floor: ₹ 108 lakh; Third Floor: ₹ 118.15 lakh; Fourth Floor: ₹ 121.35 lakh and 
External Electrification & DG Set Work: ₹ 77.35 lakh. 

Audit Report–SPSEs for the year ended 31 March 2023 
115 
without calling tender. However, there was a stipulation that no work 
should be split. KRIDL submitted (October 2017) seven separate 
estimates -one for each floor of the building.  The Company, however, 
entrusted the work to KRIDL based on estimates which were split. 
• KRIDL requested (January 2019) the Company to release 25 per cent of 
the estimate (₹ 44.43 lakh) as work valuing ₹ 82 lakh (which was more 
than 40 per cent of the estimate) was completed. As the Company did 
not release the same, KRIDL again requested ( March 2019 ) the 
Company to release funds. As the Company did not release the amount 
even in May 2019, the work was stopped.  Work valuing ₹ 111.61 lakh 
was completed till then.  EE, KRIDL once again requested ( January 
2020) the Company to release the balance amo unt and also urged the 
Company that the column steel reinforcement would get corroded if the 
concreting was not done. The Company requested ( October 2020 ) 
KRIDL to submit a revised estimate to complete the basement floor 
work to the level that ground floor could be utilised. There was no 
further progress in the work till date. 
Due to non-release of funds to KRIDL and consequent non-payment of 
bills, the work which was to be completed by March 2019 is yet to be 
completed. Even after completion of this work (basement), six more 
estimates are to be taken up which makes the timeline s for the 
completion of the building uncertain. 
The Government endorsed (March 2024) the reply of the Company which stated 
that it was decided to execute the project in a phased manner based on 
availability of funds and also due to lack of skilled personnel to monitor the 
project. Accordingly, the estimates were obtained from KRIDL.  However, the 
project could not be carried out due to the financial losses faced during the past 
years. Further, the Company decided to (during 307th BM held on 28-12-2023) 
complete the project.  
The reply is not acceptable as the splitting of work into seven estimates 
disregarding the stipulations of the exemption notification to entrust the work 
to KRIDL is not in order. The work relating to the remaining six more estimates 
have to be taken up one after the other, after completion of the basement. This 
would invariably delay the completion of the project/building. 
Hence, even after more than four years of starting the work and incurring an 
expenditure of ₹ 88.88 lakh, the objective of having a building which would 
have saved warehouse rent of ₹ 50.68 lakh annually was not fulfilled. Further, 
the partially constructed building also will deteriorate with the efflux of time. 
II. Government of Karnataka (GoK) decided (March 2007) to develop 
Shivarapatna village in Malur Taluq of Kolar District as “Sculpture Village” to 
preserve the sculptor community’s traditional stone carving skills and use this 
as the means to improve livelihood for the entire village. GoK sanctioned 
(March 2007) ₹ 1 crore to the Company for the same.  

Chapter- VI: Compliance Audit Observations on State Public Sector Enterprises 
116 
Kadambari, a Non-Government Organisation (NGO), evinced keen interest  in 
this project and came forward to be the architect and manager of the project. In 
a meeting between Development Commission for Handicrafts, Government of 
India, Rural Development & Panchayat Raj Department, the Company and  
Kadambari, it was decided (March 2008)  to take up restoration of Sri 
Varadarajaswamy Temple, Construction of Community Facility Centre among 
other works. Consequently, the works of construction of Samudaya Bhavan at 
an estimated cost of ₹ 59.50 lakh  and restoration of Sri Varadarajaswamy 
Temple at an estimated cost of ₹ 13.68 lakh were awarded (February 2009) to 
Shri K Srinivas (contractor) by the Executive Engineer, Panchayat Raj 
Engineering Division, Kolar. Government of Karnataka granted ( February 
2009) 4 (g) exemption from KTPP Act to entrust the work of architecture and 
project management to Kadambari at a cost of ₹ 10 lakh. The site was handed 
over to the contractor in October 2009 and the work was to be completed within 
nine months from the date of handing over the site (by July 2010). Based on the 
requests, the Company released ₹ 35.35 lakh138 to the Chief Executive Officer, 
Zilla Panchayat, Kolar.  
The works were delayed mainly due to the contractor. Five notices were issued 
to the contractor between June 2010 and August 2011. The contractor had 
agreed (May 2011) to complete the work by September 2011. However, the 
contractor did not complete the work. 
Kadambari stated (June 2013)  that the contractor has to correct all the quality 
and basic construction problems and make himself and his team available for 
meetings called for review of work.  The Executive Engineer, Panchayat Raj 
Engineering Division, Kolar stated ( June 2016) that the work was incomplete 
and stopped. In view of the failure of the contractor to execute the work as per 
the designs and instructions, Kadambari withdrew (June 2016) from the project. 
There was no further progress in the work.  The total expenditure of ₹ 39.04 
lakh139 incurred on the project became infructuous due to lack of co-ordination 
and monitoring. 
The Government endorsed ( March 2024 ) the reply of the Company which 
stated, that during February 2022/May 2023 , it had addressed letters to the 
Chief Executive Officer, Zilla Panchayat, Kolar calling for details of 
expenditure, utilization certificates and reasons for non-completion of the work 
even after ten years for which reply is yet to be received.  The Company also 
stated that it is in constant touch with the officials to decide on a further course 
of action. The reply reiterates the deficiency in monitoring as the Company has 
not taken any action from June 2016 to February 2022. 
Hence, the very objective of the GoK to develop the Shivarapatna village as 
“Sculpture Village” for the welfare of sculptor community thereby to improve 
livelihood for the entire village has not been fulfilled even after the completion 
of 14 years. Further, the Company’s objective to own its multi-utility complex 
to reap benefits thereon also remains stranded even after completion of 4 years.  
 
138 ₹ 13.35 lakh on 29 April 2010; ₹ 11 lakh on 28 September 2010 and ₹ 11 lakh on 18 
November 2010. 
139 Including ₹ 3.19 lakh towards printing and ₹ 0.50 lakh towards honorarium.

Thus, the Company could not achieve its objectives in the above cases in spite 
of incurring an expenditure of ₹ 1.28 crore The reasons for non -completion of 
the works were splitting of works, non-payment of contractor’s bills, lack of co-
ordination and monitoring. 
Karnataka State Police Housing and Infrastructure Development 
Corporation Limited 
6.14 Failure to enforce defect liability clause resulted in extra cost. 
Non-compliance by Karnataka State Police Housing and Infrastructure 
Development Corporation Limited to KPWD Code regarding reckoning of 
the work completion date led to non -enforcement of defect liability clause 
against the Contractor resulting in extra expenditure of ₹  1.23 crore for 
rectification of the structural defects. 
Paragraph 188 of Karnataka Public Works Departmental Code (KPWD Code) 
prescribes that Contractor should submit a completion report when the Sub -
Divisional Officer in charge of the work is satisfied that: 
• all items mentioned in the estimate or other necessary contingent items 
in respect of water supply, UGD and electricity are completed and  
• all unwanted materials etc., have been removed and the surroundings as 
well as interior of the work were made clean. 
The Executive Engineer may then take over the work from the Contractor after 
which the Contractors’ final bill may be paid and accounts of the work closed 
by submitting the completion certificate.  
Government of Karnataka approved (November 2013) the project of Karnataka 
State Police Housing and Infrastructure Development Corporation Limited (the 
Company) to take up the construction of 11,000 Police Quarters at various 
districts in Karnataka under Police Gruha 2020 Scheme.  Accordingly, the 
Company took up the work of construction of 128 police constable quarters at 
Binny Mill, Tank Bund Road in Bengaluru Urban District. The Managing 
Director (MD) of the Company accorded (December 2015) technical sanction 
to the estimate of ₹ 30.91 crore and invited (December 2015) tenders for the 
work on lumpsum turn -key basis based on tenderer’s own  design/alternate 
technology/conventional method of construction. The Company entrusted 
(April 2016) the work to M/s. P.G. Setty Constructions Technology Pvt. Ltd, 
Mysore (Contractor) at the tendered cost of ₹ 23.98 crore.  The scope of work 
Recommendation:  
27. Audit recommends that Management should comply with 
provisions of 4(g) exemptions under KTPP Act while taking up the 
works and should ensure timely completion of projects to achieve 
intended benefits besides to avoid additional financial burden.  

Chapter- VI: Compliance Audit Observations on State Public Sector Enterprises 
118 
consisted of construction of 128 flats in two blocks A and B having 64 flats 
each. The work was stipulated to be completed by 28 November 2017. 
Clause No.8 of the agreement executed (March 2016) between the Company 
and Contractor specified that:  
(i) Defect Liability Period (DLP) shall mean the period after the issue of 
certificate of completion of work by the Engineer -in-charge during 
which the structure has to function without any trouble or defects.  
(ii) DLP shall be 12 months from the date of completion as certified by the 
Engineer-in-charge except for the lift and STP for which DLP would be 
three years from the date of completion.  
(iii) During DLP, the Contractor is solely responsible for repairing / 
replacing any structural defects found in the work executed.  
Clause 31.1 of the agreement also prescribed that the employer shall give notice 
to the Contractor for any defects noticed before the end of the DLP and DLP 
shall be extended as long as the defects were not rectified. 
Audit scrutiny (November 2022) of records of the Company revealed that the 
Contractor could not complete the work within the stipulated period and hence, 
extension of time to complete the work was granted by the Company from time 
to time.  Third and last extension of time was up to 31 August 2018. The 
measurements for the final bills were carried out on 25 March 2019  and the 
final bill for up-to-date work done amounting to ₹  23.98 crore was paid to the 
Contractor on 23 May 2019 . However, the date of completi on of work was 
indicated as 31 August 2018 in the Work Completion certificate issued by the 
Executive Engineer, Bengaluru Urban-2 (Division). The Company also released 
(March 2020) the withheld amount of ₹ 0.36 crore on account of defect liability 
period and the security deposit of ₹ 1.20 crore in respect of the above work. The 
fact that the Contractor did not complete the work as of 31 August 2018 was 
substantiated by the following: 
• The Contractor in letter addressed (September 2018) to the Executive 
Engineer had admitted that final asphalting works of internal roads could 
not be completed as there was continuous rain. 
• The Project Management Consultant (PMC) 140 in the letters addressed 
(October 2018  and February 2019 ) to the Contractor had stated that 
works pertaining to road asphalting, external and internal electrical 
commissioning, plantation of trees and approval from statutory bodies 
such as Fire Department, BWSSB and BESCOM were pending. 
• Assistant Executive Engineer, No.1 Sub -division, Bengaluru in letter 
addressed (February 2019) to Executive Engineer of the Division opined 
that the final bill of the Contractor could be admitted, only if, the 
 
140 ACS Design Consulting Pvt. Ltd., Bengaluru. 

Audit Report–SPSEs for the year ended 31 March 2023 
119 
pending works141 were completed and time extension up to 17 December 
2018 was granted. 
• The PMC after scrutiny of final bill submitted (February 2019) by the 
Contractor informed (March 2019)  that final payment could be 
considered after withholding relevant amount for balance works pending 
for completion. The final bill was admitted (April 2019) for payment 
only after the balance works were attended by the Contractor. 
Thus, from the above communications between the Company, PMC and the 
Contractor, it was evident that the work was not completed till March 2019 
when the final measurements were made. Hence, the issue of work completion 
certificate indicating the date of completion as 31 August 2018 was done 
without exercising due diligence and needs justification. 
Meanwhile, the Company observed (June 2019 ) that there was separation of 
grouted joint between parapet of the corridor and lift well of Block B. During 
September 2019, Block B of the quarters showed foundation settlement which 
resulted in leaning of Block B towards east. The officials of the Company, PMC 
and the Contractor conducted (June 2020) site visit for inspection of the gap at 
joints on either side of Block B. The vertical alignment of the building at eight 
corners of Block B and the distances betwee n the plumb lines were watched 
continuously for six months which confirmed that the gap had widened further. 
The Company constituted (September 2021)  a Technical Experts Advisory 
Committee (TEAC) to obtain suitable remedial measures for widening gap 
joints on either side of the lift well in Block -B of the PC Quarters.  TEAC 
recommended (April 2022) to strengthen sub soil and existing raft of the tilting 
portion of Block B by driving micro piles with grouting and providing an 
additional thickness of reinforced concrete to the existing raft.  
Though the MD issued (October 2021) a notice to the Contractor to rectify the 
defects within a period of 15 days, action taken against or by the Contractor in 
this regard was not made available to Audit. The records further revealed that 
the Board of Directors while according approval to r estoration work (March 
2022) directed to forfeit any retained amount of the original Contractor lying 
with the Company and to explore the possibility of recovery from other 
Government works being executed by the Contractor, as well as to initiate civil 
and criminal proceedings for imposing penalty and awarding damages for 
negligence, misrepresentation of facts and fraud. The Company entrusted (July 
2022) the above work to M/s. G.P. Soil Techniques, Bengaluru for an agreed 
tendered amount of ₹ 1.23 crore. The work was under progress and payment of 
₹ 1.10 crore was made (March 2023) to M/s. G.P. Soil Techniques. 
Thus, non -compliance by the Company to the provisions of KPWD Code 
regarding determination of work completion date resulted in preponing the date 
to August 2018, even though substantial works were pending to be completed. 
Further, though the defects were i nitially noticed during June 2019 which was 
 
141 Providing water supply connection, testing raw and treated water, providing service shafts 
and vent doors for corridors, painting of kerb stones, toilet ceiling and STP pump room, 
testing of water supply and sanitary pipelines etc. 

Chapter- VI: Compliance Audit Observations on State Public Sector Enterprises 
120 
well within the DLP, the Company failed to evoke  Clause 31.1 of the contract 
agreement which stipulated extension of DLP till the defects were rectified. As 
a result, the Company failed to comply the directives of the Board of Directors 
for holding the Contractor responsible for the defects in construction and had to 
incur extra expenditure of ₹ 1.23 crore for rectification of the structural defects. 
The structural defects rendered the tilted portion of Block B consisting of 32 
flats unusable till the rectification works were completed. 
The Company replied (March 2024) that major structural defects were noticed 
after the DLP, the pending works carried out during DLP were minor in nature 
and that final bills were submitted after due completion of the above pending 
works. The Company also stated that though the defect was initially observed 
during June 2019, the Contractor conceived it as a separation of joint and 
covered up the structural defects t o absolve his responsibility during DLP. 
Hence, the Company has filed a criminal case (March 2022) and a civil suit of 
₹ 15.80 crore ( October 2023) against the Contractor to compensate for the 
financial loss and collateral damages which included ₹ 1.25 crore incurred 
towards restoration work. Government endorsed (March 2024) the reply of the 
Company. 
The reply is not acceptable due to the following reasons: 
• The pending works as of August 2018 were not minor but a part of the 
original scope of work as evidenced by the letters submitted by AEE and 
PMC in charge of the work and were required to be completed before 
issuing the completion certificate as per Paragraph 188 of KPWD Code; 
• The Company not only failed to extend the DLP as per Clause 31.1 of 
the Contract agreement, but also released (March 2020) the available 
security deposit (₹ 1.20 crore) and money withheld (₹ 0.36 crore) from 
the Contractor despite having noticed major structural defects in the 
construction of police quarters in June 2019 itself.  
Thus incorrect determination of work completion date and consequent failure 
to operate the defect liability clause and fix responsibility on the Contractor for 
the structural defects in t he building  has resulted in financial loss to the 
Company on account of extra expenditure of ₹ 1.23 crore for rectification 
works. 
 
 
 
Recommendation:  
28. Responsibility needs to be fixed on the officials who were 
responsible for flouting KPWD code provisions regarding 
determination of work completion date and contract clauses 
regarding operation of the defect liability period.

Mysore Sales International Limited 
6.15 Avoidable Expenditure 
Delay in execution of the project of construction of Godown building 
resulted in avoidable expenditure of ₹ 93.14 lakh. 
The Mysore Sales International Limited (Company) approached (June 2002) 
Karnataka Industrial Area Development Board (KIADB) for allotment of one 
acre land for construction of Godown to construct a warehouse for paper and 
liquor Divisions at Kalaburgi (erstwhile Gulbarga).  The KIADB allotted (July 
2002) the land (plot numbers 30 and 31 measuring 3,873 sq. metres) at Kapnoor 
2nd Stage Industrial Area, Kalaburgi at a tentative price of ₹ 4.00 lakh.  The 
possession of the land was taken (August 2007) and lease cum sale agreement 
was entered into with KIADB (October 2007). As per the terms and conditions 
of lease cum sale agreement, the lease would be for a period of six years with a 
condition that the Company should complete construction work within 24 
months from the date of taking possession of the scheduled premises.  
The Company, during January 2011, decided to take  up the construction of 
Godown at Kalaburgi and appointed (September 2012) an architect for 
preparation of estimates and structural designs.  The architect prepared the 
estimate at ₹ 7.59 crore for construction of ground and first floor. Based on the 
estimate, the Company initiated the tender process in December 2012  but the 
tenders were cancelled  after evaluation stating that the project would be 
implemented after completing the ongoing projects at Baikampady, Mangaluru 
and Karnataka Bhavan at Mumbai.  The construction was deferred up to 2017 
and during 2018, Company appointed a consultant civil engineer, who prepared 
an estimate of ₹ 3.00 crore for ground floor. The Company invited (December 
2018) tenders for construction of the warehouse and Work Order was issued  
(July 2019) to lowest tenderer M/s. S & S Innovations (contractor) at negotiated 
price of ₹ 2.90 crore with a stipulation to complete the work in 14 months i.e. 
by September 2020. However, the work was not completed and a revised work 
order with additional cost of ₹ 1.88 crore was issued in November 2021 . The 
work which was to be completed by May 2022 (as per revised work order) is 
yet to be completed (September 2023). 
In this connection, Audit observed that: 
• There was inordinate delay in commencing the project. As per clause 
10(1)(iii) of the lease cum sale agreement, lessee shall complete 
construction work within 24 months from the date of  taking possession 
of the land  (July 2009).  As the Company could not take up the 
construction of the Godown within the stipulated time, extension of time 
was requested and KIADB granted extension  periodically by levying 
penalty and collecting current market cost of land.  The Company paid 
difference in land cost amounting to ₹ 15.15 lakh in August 2013 and a 
penalty of ₹ 2 lakh in February 2016.  

Chapter- VI: Compliance Audit Observations on State Public Sector Enterprises 
122 
• The plan sanctioned (March 2013) by KIADB had provision for ground 
floor and first floor.  The consultant Civil Engineer appointed by 
Company for the project prepared the estimate for ₹ 3 crore without 
proper drawings and limiting the project to ground floor only and 
limiting the quantities of some key items like excavation, columns and 
beams. The Company invited tenders based on the estimate prepared by 
the engineer and issued Work Order in July 2019 . However, as the 
executed quantities did not match with the estimated quantities, the 
architects142 prepared (October 2020) a revised estimate for ₹ 4.88 crore 
and a revised work order with additional cost of ₹ 1.88 crore was issued 
in November 2021 after approval of the Board (September 2021).  The 
extra items included, excavation in soft and hard rock, construction of 
embankment and providing water proof treatment to roof besides 
construction of head room. Further, the contractor/architect did not seek 
prior approval of the Company for carrying out the additional work  
which involved additional cost of ₹ 1.88 crore. Thus, preparation of 
defective estimate by the consultant engineer resulted in cost escalation 
and further delay in completion of the project.  
• Considering three months (from January 2013) for feasibility and two 
years as the time required for finalization of tenders and for execution 
of the work, the Company could have completed the construction of the 
building by March 2016 and it could have occ upied the premises, 
thereby avoiding payment of rent for its office and godown.  The 
avoidable payment of rent for the period from April 2016 to September 
2023 works out to ₹ 75.99 lakh as detailed in Appendix - 39.  
• During construction, soft rock (1727.56 cum) and hard rock (854.70 
cum) were excavated as extra items and an amount of ₹ 42.00 lakh was 
paid. However, the same were neither accounted for nor utilised.  
The Government replied (January 2024) that the Company could not take up the 
project within extended time, as it was in the process of completing important 
projects (such as Karnataka Bhavan in Navi Mumbai, Warehouse at Mangalore) 
and due to administrative reasons.  The reply is not acceptable as the Godown 
was constructed without completion of the Karnataka Bhavan at Navi Mumbai, 
which was also delayed 143 and the Mangaluru Godown was completed and 
leased only during June 2022.  The exact administrative reasons have not been 
explained to Audit.  
 
142 M/s. Shobha & Mahesh Associates. 
143 Commented in Para 2.3 of Part-II, Report no. 3 of the year 2022, Government of Karnataka. 

Audit Report–SPSEs for the year ended 31 March 2023 
123 
Thus, the deferment of commencing the work of construction of building even  
though estimate was prepared, tenders were floated and building plan was 
approved by KIADB, as early as in 2013-14, resulted in incurring an avoidable 
expenditure of ₹ 93.14 lakh144.
